      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Monster danau ini kebanyakan digambarkan sebagai makhluk menyerupai ular berkulit gelap licin dengan tubuh besar lebih tebal daripada tiang telepon dan panjang hingga 15 m.",
        "en": "The lake monster has mostly been described as a serpentine creature with smooth dark skin and a large body thicker than a telephone pole, up to 15 m (49 ft) long."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c04",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s1",
      "quote": "The Naitaka was often described as using its tail to create fierce storms to drown victims.",
      "locator": "Native origins",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Naitaka kerap digambarkan memakai ekornya untuk menimbulkan badai dahsyat guna menenggelamkan korban.",
        "en": "The Naitaka was often described as using its tail to create fierce storms to drown victims."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c05",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s1",
      "quote": "The monster has said to move at incredible speeds, coiling its body in vertical undulations, and propelling itself with a powerful tail.",
      "locator": "Background",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Monster itu dikatakan bergerak dengan kecepatan luar biasa, melingkarkan tubuhnya dalam gelombang vertikal, dan melaju dengan ekornya yang kuat.",
        "en": "The monster is said to move at incredible speeds, coiling its body in vertical undulations and propelling itself with a powerful tail."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c06",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s1",
      "quote": "According to historian Mark M. Orkin, the creature received its colonial name \"on a night in 1924 when the strains of an English music-hall song were first heard in the city of Vernon, British Columbia\". Orkin cites the following lines from the song: ... And Ogopogo was his name.",
      "locator": "Etymology",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Menurut sejarawan Mark M. Orkin, makhluk ini menerima nama kolonialnya \"pada suatu malam tahun 1924 ketika alunan lagu music-hall Inggris pertama kali terdengar di kota Vernon, British Columbia\"; larik lagu yang ia kutip diakhiri \"And Ogopogo was his name\".",
        "en": "According to historian Mark M. Orkin the creature received its colonial name \"on a night in 1924 when the strains of an English music-hall song were first heard in the city of Vernon, British Columbia\", and the song lines he cites end \"And Ogopogo was his name\"."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c07",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s1",
      "quote": "According to Ben Radford, the Ogopogo is \"more closely tied to native myths than is any other lake monster.\" The Secwepemc and Syilx natives regarded the Ogopogo, which they called the Naitaka, as \"an evil supernatural entity with great power and ill intent.\"",
      "locator": "Native origins",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Menurut Ben Radford, Ogopogo \"lebih erat terikat pada mitos pribumi daripada monster danau mana pun\". Orang Secwepemc dan Syilx menganggap Ogopogo, yang mereka sebut Naitaka, sebagai \"entitas gaib jahat dengan kekuatan besar dan niat buruk\".",
        "en": "According to Ben Radford the Ogopogo is \"more closely tied to native myths than is any other lake monster\"; the Secwepemc and Syilx natives regarded it, calling it the Naitaka, as \"an evil supernatural entity with great power and ill intent\"."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c08",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s1",
      "quote": "In native lore, Naitaka demanded a live sacrifice for safe crossing of the lake. For hundreds of years, First Nations would sacrifice small animals before entering the water.",
      "locator": "Native origins",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam tradisi pribumi, Naitaka menuntut persembahan hidup agar penyeberangan danau aman. Selama ratusan tahun, orang First Nations mengorbankan hewan kecil sebelum memasuki air.",
        "en": "In native lore Naitaka demanded a live sacrifice for safe crossing of the lake, and for hundreds of years First Nations would sacrifice small animals before entering the water."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c09",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s1",
      "quote": "Oral traditions often described visiting chief Timbasket, who rejected the required sacrifice, denying the existence of the demon. Upon entering the lake on a canoe with his family, Naitaka \"whipped up the surface of the lake with his long tail\" and the canoe and its occupants were sucked to the bottom of the lake.",
      "locator": "Native origins",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Tradisi lisan sering menceritakan kepala suku tamu Timbasket yang menolak persembahan yang disyaratkan dan menyangkal keberadaan iblis itu. Ketika ia memasuki danau dengan kano bersama keluarganya, Naitaka \"mencambuk permukaan danau dengan ekor panjangnya\" dan kano beserta penumpangnya terhisap ke dasar danau.",
        "en": "Oral traditions often described the visiting chief Timbasket, who rejected the required sacrifice and denied the demon's existence; when he entered the lake by canoe with his family, Naitaka \"whipped up the surface of the lake with his long tail\" and the canoe and its occupants were sucked to the bottom."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c10",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s1",
      "quote": "According to Pat Raphael of the Westbank First Nation, a member nation of the larger Okanagan Nation Alliance, the demonic view of Naitaka came about through miscommunication between Canada's early European settlers and the Syilx/Okanagan people. To the Syilx, it's ... a sacred spirit of the lake that protects the valley.",
      "locator": "Native origins",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Menurut Pat Raphael dari Westbank First Nation, pandangan yang menganggap Naitaka iblis muncul dari salah komunikasi antara pemukim Eropa awal di Kanada dan orang Syilx/Okanagan. Bagi orang Syilx, ia adalah roh suci danau yang melindungi lembah.",
        "en": "According to Pat Raphael of the Westbank First Nation, the demonic view of Naitaka arose from miscommunication between early European settlers and the Syilx/Okanagan people; to the Syilx it is a sacred spirit of the lake that protects the valley."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c11",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s1",
      "quote": "However, Jordan Coble of the Westbank First Nation counters by saying that the creature is a being that is meant to be both respected, not feared.",
      "locator": "Native origins",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Namun Jordan Coble dari Westbank First Nation menyanggah dengan mengatakan bahwa makhluk itu adalah sosok yang dimaksudkan untuk dihormati, bukan ditakuti.",
        "en": "Jordan Coble of the Westbank First Nation counters that the creature is a being meant to be respected, not feared."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c12",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s1",
      "quote": "The Naitaka was often described as using its tail to create fierce storms to drown victims. In 1855, settler John MacDougal claimed that his horses were sucked down into the water, and nearly his canoe before he cut the line.",
      "locator": "Native origins",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Naitaka kerap digambarkan memakai ekornya untuk menimbulkan badai dahsyat guna menenggelamkan korban. Pada 1855, pemukim John MacDougal mengaku kudanya terhisap ke dalam air, dan hampir kanonya sebelum ia memotong talinya.",
        "en": "The Naitaka was often described as using its tail to create fierce storms to drown victims; in 1855 settler John MacDougal claimed his horses were sucked down into the water, and nearly his canoe before he cut the line."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c13",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s1",
      "quote": "In the 1980s, a local tourism agency offered a cash reward for a proven sighting of the beast. Greenpeace announced that the beast must be filmed and not captured: the Ogopogo was listed as an endangered species.",
      "locator": "Alleged sightings",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Pada 1980-an, sebuah lembaga pariwisata setempat menawarkan hadiah uang tunai bagi penampakan makhluk itu yang terbukti. Greenpeace menyatakan makhluk itu harus difilmkan dan tidak ditangkap: Ogopogo didaftarkan sebagai spesies terancam.",
        "en": "In the 1980s a local tourism agency offered a cash reward for a proven sighting of the beast, and Greenpeace announced it must be filmed and not captured, listing the Ogopogo as an endangered species."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c14",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s1",
      "quote": "In 2011, a cell phone video captured two dark shapes in the water. A suggested explanation is that the video shows two logs.",
      "locator": "Alleged sightings",
      "context": "scholarly-interpretation",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Pada 2011, sebuah video ponsel merekam dua bentuk gelap di air. Penjelasan yang diajukan adalah bahwa video itu menunjukkan dua batang kayu.",
        "en": "In 2011 a cell phone video captured two dark shapes in the water; a suggested explanation is that it shows two logs."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c15",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s2",
      "quote": "Ogopogo is the name of a legendary lake monster that is said to live in Okanagan Lake in the Canadian province of British Columbia. Stories of a large creature living in the lake go back hundreds of years and were told by the region’s First Nations people who believed it to be a sacred lake spirit.",
      "locator": "Overview",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ogopogo adalah nama monster danau legendaris yang dikatakan hidup di Danau Okanagan di provinsi British Columbia, Kanada. Kisah tentang makhluk besar di danau itu sudah berusia ratusan tahun dan dituturkan orang First Nations setempat yang meyakininya sebagai roh danau yang suci.",
        "en": "Ogopogo is a legendary lake monster said to live in Okanagan Lake in British Columbia, and stories of a large creature in the lake go back hundreds of years, told by the region's First Nations people who believed it to be a sacred lake spirit."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c16",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s2",
      "quote": "While eyewitness accounts vary, most describe Ogopogo as a snake-like creature about 80-feet (24-meters) long with a head resembling a horse or a goat.",
      "locator": "Overview",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Meskipun kesaksian berbeda-beda, kebanyakan menggambarkan Ogopogo sebagai makhluk menyerupai ular sepanjang sekitar 80 kaki (24 meter) dengan kepala mirip kuda atau kambing.",
        "en": "While eyewitness accounts vary, most describe Ogopogo as a snake-like creature about 80 feet (24 meters) long with a head resembling a horse or a goat."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c17",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s2",
      "quote": "To the Syilx, the creature was known as N’ha-a-itk, which means “sacred spirit of the lake.” N’ha-a-itk was said to be dark in color with the head of a horse and the antlers of a deer. According to local legend, N’ha-a-itk was a spirit that could also take physical form.",
      "locator": "Background",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Bagi orang Syilx, makhluk itu dikenal sebagai N'ha-a-itk, yang berarti \"roh suci danau\". N'ha-a-itk dikatakan berwarna gelap dengan kepala kuda dan tanduk rusa. Menurut legenda setempat, N'ha-a-itk adalah roh yang juga dapat mengambil wujud fisik.",
        "en": "To the Syilx the creature was known as N'ha-a-itk, meaning \"sacred spirit of the lake\", said to be dark with the head of a horse and the antlers of a deer, and according to local legend a spirit that could also take physical form."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c18",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s2",
      "quote": "It was said to live in a cave near an island and required a sacrifice to allow the safe passage of the lake. The people were said to bring chickens, salmon, and other small animals with them to toss into the lake to appease N’ha-a-itk.",
      "locator": "Background",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Makhluk itu dikatakan tinggal di gua dekat sebuah pulau dan menuntut persembahan agar penyeberangan danau aman. Orang-orang dikatakan membawa ayam, salmon, dan hewan kecil lain untuk dilemparkan ke danau guna menenangkan N'ha-a-itk.",
        "en": "It was said to live in a cave near an island and required a sacrifice for safe passage on the lake; people were said to bring chickens, salmon, and other small animals to toss into the lake to appease N'ha-a-itk."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c19",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s2",
      "quote": "The first recorded sighting by a settler occurred in 1855, when a man claimed that something dragged his horses underwater as he tried to cross the lake. In 1872, a Canadian author named Susan Allison reported that she saw a dinosaur swimming in Okanagan Lake.",
      "locator": "Background",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Penampakan pertama yang tercatat oleh seorang pemukim terjadi pada 1855, ketika seorang pria mengaku ada sesuatu yang menyeret kudanya ke bawah air saat ia menyeberangi danau. Pada 1872, penulis Kanada bernama Susan Allison melaporkan melihat seekor dinosaurus berenang di Danau Okanagan.",
        "en": "The first recorded sighting by a settler occurred in 1855, when a man claimed something dragged his horses underwater as he tried to cross the lake; in 1872 a Canadian author, Susan Allison, reported seeing a dinosaur swimming in Okanagan Lake."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c20",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s2",
      "quote": "In the 1920s, local tourism officials decided to call the creature Ogopogo, a name taken from a popular English folk song The Ogo-Pogo.",
      "locator": "Background",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Pada 1920-an, pejabat pariwisata setempat memutuskan menamai makhluk itu Ogopogo, nama yang diambil dari lagu rakyat Inggris populer The Ogo-Pogo.",
        "en": "In the 1920s local tourism officials decided to call the creature Ogopogo, a name taken from a popular English folk song, The Ogo-Pogo."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c21",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s2",
      "quote": "During the 1980s, an Okanagan Lake tourism association offered a one-million-dollar reward for anyone who could provide “living proof” that Ogopogo really existed.",
      "locator": "Background",
      "context": "modern-popular-culture",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Pada 1980-an, sebuah asosiasi pariwisata Danau Okanagan menawarkan hadiah satu juta dolar bagi siapa pun yang dapat memberikan \"bukti hidup\" bahwa Ogopogo benar-benar ada.",
        "en": "During the 1980s an Okanagan Lake tourism association offered a one-million-dollar reward for anyone who could provide \"living proof\" that Ogopogo really existed."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c22",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s2",
      "quote": "In 1991, a scientific expedition conducted a high-tech search of the lake for evidence that it was home to a giant aquatic creature. The expedition included an underwater remote-controlled vehicle and a submarine, but no sign of an Ogopogo was discovered.",
      "locator": "Background",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Pada 1991, sebuah ekspedisi ilmiah mencari bukti di danau dengan teknologi tinggi bahwa danau itu rumah bagi makhluk air raksasa. Ekspedisi itu memakai kendaraan bawah air kendali jarak jauh dan kapal selam, tetapi tak ada tanda Ogopogo ditemukan.",
        "en": "In 1991 a scientific expedition made a high-tech search of the lake for evidence of a giant aquatic creature, using an underwater remote-controlled vehicle and a submarine, but no sign of an Ogopogo was discovered."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c23",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s2",
      "quote": "A more logical explanation is that the “monster” may be a large fish such as a sturgeon. ... An even more plausible explanation is that Ogopogo may be nothing more than a phenomenon caused by the lake’s geology.",
      "locator": "Background",
      "context": "scholarly-interpretation",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Penjelasan yang lebih logis adalah bahwa \"monster\" itu mungkin ikan besar seperti sturgeon. Penjelasan yang lebih masuk akal lagi adalah bahwa Ogopogo mungkin hanyalah fenomena akibat geologi danau.",
        "en": "A more logical explanation is that the \"monster\" may be a large fish such as a sturgeon, and an even more plausible one is that Ogopogo may be nothing more than a phenomenon caused by the lake's geology."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c24",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s2",
      "quote": "The creature was most often said to be multi-humped with the head of a horse and green, black, or gray skin. Other reports claimed that it had the head of a goat, a snake, or even an alligator.",
      "locator": "Background",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Makhluk itu paling sering dikatakan berpunuk banyak dengan kepala kuda dan kulit hijau, hitam, atau abu-abu. Laporan lain menyebut kepalanya seperti kambing, ular, atau bahkan aligator.",
        "en": "The creature was most often said to be multi-humped with the head of a horse and green, black, or gray skin; other reports claimed the head of a goat, a snake, or even an alligator."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c25",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s1",
      "quote": "Additionally, the creature may sometimes be referred to by the pet name Oggy. Smaller creatures may be referred to as Ogopups.",
      "locator": "Etymology",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Selain itu, makhluk ini kadang disebut dengan nama sayang Oggy. Makhluk yang lebih kecil dapat disebut Ogopup.",
        "en": "The creature may sometimes be referred to by the pet name Oggy, and smaller creatures may be referred to as Ogopups."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c26",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s2",
      "quote": "Stories of the lake monster were featured on American television shows such as Unsolved Mysteries and In Search Of.",
      "locator": "Background",
      "context": "modern-popular-culture",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah monster danau itu diangkat dalam acara televisi Amerika seperti Unsolved Mysteries dan In Search Of.",
        "en": "Stories of the lake monster were featured on American television shows such as Unsolved Mysteries and In Search Of."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c27",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s1",
      "quote": "The Ogopogo now plays a role in the commercial symbolism and media representation of the region.",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "modern-popular-culture",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ogopogo kini berperan dalam simbol komersial dan representasi media kawasan itu.",
        "en": "The Ogopogo now plays a role in the commercial symbolism and media representation of the region."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c28",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s2",
      "quote": "Similar to Scotland’s famed Loch Ness monster, Ogopogo has become a local tourist attraction and even inspired a one-million-dollar bounty for proof of its existence.",
      "locator": "Overview",
      "context": "modern-popular-culture",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Seperti monster Loch Ness yang terkenal di Skotlandia, Ogopogo menjadi atraksi wisata setempat dan bahkan memicu hadiah satu juta dolar bagi bukti keberadaannya.",
        "en": "Like Scotland's famed Loch Ness monster, Ogopogo has become a local tourist attraction and even inspired a one-million-dollar bounty for proof of its existence."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c29",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s1",
      "quote": "The word \"n'ha-a-itk\" has various translations, such as \"water-demon\", \"water god\", or \"sacred creature of the water\".",
      "locator": "Native origins",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kata \"n'ha-a-itk\" punya beberapa terjemahan, seperti \"iblis air\", \"dewa air\", atau \"makhluk suci air\".",
        "en": "The word \"n'ha-a-itk\" has various translations, such as \"water-demon\", \"water god\", or \"sacred creature of the water\"."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c30",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s2",
      "quote": "As outsiders began to move into the region in the nineteenth century, they heard stories of N’ha-a-itk and interpreted the spirit as a giant snake-like monster.",
      "locator": "Background",
      "context": "scholarly-interpretation",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ketika orang luar mulai pindah ke kawasan itu pada abad ke-19, mereka mendengar kisah tentang N'ha-a-itk dan menafsirkan roh itu sebagai monster raksasa menyerupai ular.",
        "en": "As outsiders began to move into the region in the nineteenth century they heard stories of N'ha-a-itk and interpreted the spirit as a giant snake-like monster."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ogopogo-c31",
      "source_id": "ogopogo-s1",
      "quote": "The spirit was said to dwell in caves under Rattlesnake Island (a.k.a. Monster Island) or adjacent to Squally Point.",
      "locator": "Native origins",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Roh itu dikatakan berdiam di gua-gua di bawah Rattlesnake Island (alias Monster Island) atau di dekat Squally Point.",
        "en": "The spirit was said to dwell in caves under Rattlesnake Island (a.k.a. Monster Island) or adjacent to Squally Point."
      }
    }
  ],

    {

      },
      "positions": [
        {
          "summary": {
            "id": "Menurut Orkin, nama itu diterima pada suatu malam 1924 ketika lagu music-hall Inggris terdengar di Vernon.",
            "en": "According to Orkin the name came on a night in 1924 when an English music-hall song was heard in Vernon."
          },
          "claim_ids": [
            "ogopogo-c06"
          ]
        },
        {
          "summary": {

    "id": "Sedna adalah dewi laut dan hewan laut dalam agama Inuit, yang menurut mitos asal-usulnya dulu seorang gadis yang jarinya dipotong lalu menjadi penguasa hewan laut.",
    "en": "In Inuit religion Sedna is the sea goddess and mistress of marine animals; in her origin myth she was a girl whose fingers were cut off before she became ruler of the sea animals.",

  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Sedna, juga disebut Sanna, Nuliajuk, Nerrivik, Ibu Laut, atau Wanita Laut, adalah dewi laut dan hewan laut dalam agama Inuit. Sebagian besar namanya adalah cara menghindari penyebutan nama asli sosok yang ditakuti atau patut dihormati. Kisahnya adalah mitos penciptaan yang menjelaskan bagaimana ia menguasai Adlivun, padanan dunia bawah dalam kepercayaan Inuit.",
      "en": "Sedna, also called Sanna, Nuliajuk, Nerrivik, Mother of the Sea, or Sea Woman, presides over the sea and its animals in Inuit religion. Most of her names are ways of avoiding the proper name of one who is feared or respected. Her story is a creation myth explaining how she came to rule Adlivun, the Inuit counterpart of the underworld.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "sedna-c01",
        "sedna-c02",
        "sedna-c03",
        "sedna-c05"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Dalam mitos asal-usul, Sedna dulunya seorang gadis yang dilempar dari kapal. Dalam varian yang lebih sering, ia lebih dulu ditipu menikahi petrel berwujud manusia; dalam varian lain ia dipaksa menikah dengan anjing dan anak-anaknya menjadi bangsa-bangsa manusia.",
      "en": "In the origin myth Sedna was once a girl thrown from a boat. Her fingers were cut off as she clung on, and the segments became sea mammals such as small seals, big bearded seals, and walruses; in another order whales come first. In the more common variant she was first fooled into marrying a petrel in human form; in another she was forced to marry a dog and her children became the peoples of humankind.",
      "claim_ids": [

        "sedna-c12",

        "sedna-c14"

    {
      "id": "Menurut kepercayaan Inuit, Wanita Laut dapat menahan hewan laut bila pantangan perburuan, kelahiran, dan kematian dilanggar. Di Greenland diceritakan pelanggaran itu menjadi kotoran di rambutnya, dan karena tak berjari ia tak dapat menyisirnya; dukun menempuh perjalanan kepadanya, melawannya, lalu menyisir rambutnya sebelum ia membebaskan hewan-hewan laut.       "en": "According to Inuit belief the Sea Woman could withhold the sea animals when hunting, birth, and death taboos were broken. In Greenland the transgressions were said to become dirt in her hair, and having no fingers she could not comb it; a shaman travelled to her, fought her, and combed her hair before she set the sea animals free. On Baffin Island great feasts included the ritual killing of Sedna. In art she is usually pictured as a woman above the waist with the tail of a marine mammal, rather like a mermaid.",

    "id": "Anjing laut dan hewan laut lain menjadi dasar kehidupan hampir semua orang Inuit, sehingga hubungan dengan Wanita Laut penting tetapi sangat peka: ia tidak hanya menguasai hewan-hewan itu, mereka berasal darinya.   "claims": [
    {
      "id": "sedna-c01",
      "source_id": "sedna-s1",
      "quote": "Sedna (Inuktitut: ᓴᓐᓇ, romanized: Sanna , previously Sedna or Sidne) is the goddess of the sea and marine animals in Inuit religion, also known as the Mother of the Sea or Mistress of the Sea.",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sedna (Inuktitut: ᓴᓐᓇ, Sanna; sebelumnya Sedna atau Sidne) adalah dewi laut dan hewan laut dalam agama Inuit, juga dikenal sebagai Ibu Laut atau Nyonya Laut.",
        "en": "Sedna (Inuktitut: ᓴᓐᓇ, Sanna; previously Sedna or Sidne) is the goddess of the sea and marine animals in Inuit religion, also known as the Mother of the Sea or Mistress of the Sea."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sedna-c02",
      "source_id": "sedna-s1",
      "quote": "The story of Sedna, which is a creation myth, describes how she came to rule over Adlivun, the Inuit equivalent of the underworld.",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah Sedna, yang merupakan mitos penciptaan, menceritakan bagaimana ia sampai menguasai Adlivun, padanan dunia bawah dalam kepercayaan Inuit.",
        "en": "The story of Sedna, a creation myth, describes how she came to rule over Adlivun, the Inuit equivalent of the underworld."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sedna-c03",
      "source_id": "sedna-s1",
      "quote": "Most of her names are avoidance speech workarounds, due to a custom of avoiding the proper name of one fearful or deserving of respect. ... Sanna (ᓴᓐᓇ ) meaning \"the great one down below\". Sedna is an older spelling.",
      "locator": "Names",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sebagian besar namanya adalah penghindaran penyebutan nama asli, karena kebiasaan menghindari nama sebenarnya dari sosok yang ditakuti atau patut dihormati. Sanna berarti \"yang agung di bawah sana\", dan Sedna adalah ejaan yang lebih tua.",
        "en": "Most of her names are avoidance-speech workarounds, owing to a custom of avoiding the proper name of one who is feared or deserves respect; Sanna means \"the great one down below\" and Sedna is an older spelling."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sedna-c04",
      "source_id": "sedna-s2",
      "quote": "An origin myth tells how Sedna was once a girl who was thrown overboard from a vessel. While she tried to hang on, her fingers were cut off at the joints. She sank to the bottom of the sea, the segments of her fingers turning into sea mammals, and she became the Sea Woman, who was in control of these animals.",
      "locator": "Sedna",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sebuah mitos asal-usul menceritakan bahwa Sedna dulunya seorang gadis yang dilempar dari kapal. Saat ia berpegangan, jari-jarinya dipotong di ruas-ruasnya. Ia tenggelam ke dasar laut, ruas-ruas jarinya menjadi mamalia laut, dan ia menjadi Wanita Laut (Sea Woman) yang menguasai hewan-hewan itu.",
        "en": "An origin myth tells how Sedna was once a girl thrown overboard from a vessel; her fingers were cut off at the joints as she held on, the segments turned into sea mammals, and she sank to the sea bottom and became the Sea Woman, who controlled these animals."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sedna-c05",
      "source_id": "sedna-s2",
      "quote": "Franz Boas, in his monograph about the Inuit on Baffin Island (1888), gave her name as Sedna, which probably means \"the one down there.\" Other Inuit groups referred to the Sea Woman under different names, such as Nerrivik (Polar Inuit, \"the place of the food\") and Nuliajuk (Netsilik Inuit, \"the lubricious one\").",
      "locator": "Sedna",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Franz Boas, dalam monografnya tentang orang Inuit di Pulau Baffin (1888), menuliskan namanya sebagai Sedna, yang kemungkinan berarti \"yang di bawah sana\". Kelompok Inuit lain menyebut Wanita Laut dengan nama berbeda, misalnya Nerrivik (Inuit Kutub, \"tempat makanan\") dan Nuliajuk (Inuit Netsilik, \"si birahi\").",
        "en": "Franz Boas, in his 1888 monograph on the Inuit of Baffin Island, gave her name as Sedna, which probably means \"the one down there\"; other Inuit groups used other names for the Sea Woman, such as Nerrivik (Polar Inuit, \"the place of the food\") and Nuliajuk (Netsilik Inuit, \"the lubricious one\")."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sedna-c06",
      "source_id": "sedna-s2",
      "quote": "she became the Sea Woman, who was in control of these animals. According to some Iglulik Inuit and Baffin Islanders, she also ruled over the souls of those who had gone to the undersea land of the dead.",
      "locator": "Sedna",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia menjadi Wanita Laut yang menguasai hewan-hewan itu. Menurut sebagian orang Iglulik Inuit dan penduduk Baffin, ia juga menguasai jiwa orang-orang yang telah pergi ke negeri orang mati di bawah laut.",
        "en": "She became the Sea Woman, who controlled these animals, and according to some Iglulik Inuit and Baffin Islanders she also ruled over the souls of those who had gone to the undersea land of the dead."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sedna-c07",
      "source_id": "sedna-s2",
      "quote": "In some variants the girl's father forces her to marry a dog because she has refused to marry. Her children become Indians, white people, and so on. In this way the mother of the sea animals is made the mother of men as well; as such, she represents the female principle of the world.",
      "locator": "Sedna",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam sebagian varian, ayah sang gadis memaksanya menikah dengan anjing karena ia menolak menikah. Anak-anaknya menjadi orang Indian, orang kulit putih, dan seterusnya. Dengan demikian ibu hewan laut juga dijadikan ibu manusia dan melambangkan prinsip feminin dunia.",
        "en": "In some variants the girl's father forces her to marry a dog because she refused to marry; her children become Indians, white people, and so on, so the mother of the sea animals is made the mother of men as well and represents the female principle of the world."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sedna-c08",
      "source_id": "sedna-s2",
      "quote": "According to Inuit belief, the Sea Woman had the power to withhold the sea animals when certain hunting, birth, and death taboos had been broken. In Greenland it was told that the transgressions would materialize as dirt in the Sea Woman's hair, making her feel uncomfortable; because she had no fingers, she was unable to comb her hair.",
      "locator": "Sedna",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Menurut kepercayaan Inuit, Wanita Laut berkuasa menahan hewan laut bila pantangan tertentu tentang perburuan, kelahiran, dan kematian dilanggar. Di Greenland diceritakan bahwa pelanggaran itu menjadi kotoran di rambutnya hingga ia tidak nyaman; karena tak berjari, ia tidak dapat menyisir rambutnya.",
        "en": "According to Inuit belief the Sea Woman could withhold the sea animals when certain hunting, birth, and death taboos were broken; in Greenland the transgressions were said to become dirt in her hair, making her uncomfortable, and having no fingers she could not comb it."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sedna-c09",
      "source_id": "sedna-s2",
      "quote": "Before the shaman could comb Sedna's hair he had to fight her. Afterward, she would set the sea animals free, and the shaman would return",
      "locator": "Sedna",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sebelum dapat menyisir rambut Sedna, sang dukun harus melawannya. Setelah itu ia membebaskan hewan-hewan laut, dan sang dukun kembali.",
        "en": "Before the shaman could comb Sedna's hair he had to fight her; afterward she would set the sea animals free and the shaman would return."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sedna-c10",
      "source_id": "sedna-s2",
      "quote": "The Inuit on Baffin Island held great feasts, lasting several days, in which Sedna was ritually killed. These calendar feasts took place in the autumn when sealing was prevented by storms that broke the ice open.",
      "locator": "Sedna",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Orang Inuit di Pulau Baffin menggelar pesta besar berhari-hari yang di dalamnya Sedna dibunuh secara ritual. Pesta kalender ini berlangsung pada musim gugur ketika badai memecahkan es sehingga perburuan anjing laut terhalang.",
        "en": "The Inuit on Baffin Island held great multi-day feasts in which Sedna was ritually killed; these calendar feasts took place in autumn, when storms breaking the ice prevented sealing."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sedna-c11",
      "source_id": "sedna-s2",
      "quote": "Seals and other sea animals were the basis of the existence of nearly all Inuit. The relationship with the Sea Woman was therefore important but very sensitive: she not only controlled these animals, they originated from her.",
      "locator": "Sedna",
      "context": "scholarly-interpretation",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Anjing laut dan hewan laut lain menjadi dasar kehidupan hampir semua orang Inuit. Karena itu hubungan dengan Wanita Laut penting tetapi sangat peka: ia tidak hanya menguasai hewan-hewan itu, mereka berasal darinya.",
        "en": "Seals and other sea animals were the basis of nearly all Inuit existence, so the relationship with the Sea Woman was important but very sensitive: she not only controlled these animals, they originated from her."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sedna-c12",
      "source_id": "sedna-s2",
      "quote": "Variants of this myth have been recorded from many localities in Greenland and Canada, but from Alaska only a single reference exists. In some variants an orphan girl is thrown overboard, but more often the myth begins with the story of a girl who was fooled into marrying a petrel that had taken on human form.",
      "locator": "Sedna",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Varian mitos ini tercatat dari banyak tempat di Greenland dan Kanada, tetapi dari Alaska hanya ada satu rujukan. Dalam sebagian varian seorang gadis yatim dilempar dari kapal, tetapi lebih sering mitos dimulai dengan gadis yang ditipu menikahi seekor petrel yang berwujud manusia.",
        "en": "Variants of the myth have been recorded from many localities in Greenland and Canada but from Alaska only one reference exists; in some an orphan girl is thrown overboard, but more often a girl is fooled into marrying a petrel that had taken human form."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sedna-c13",
      "source_id": "sedna-s2",
      "quote": "The father tried to pacify the petrel by throwing his daughter overboard. When she tried to cling to the side of the boat, her father cut off her fingers at the first joint. Her fingertips fell into the sea and became small seals. When she again grasped the side of the boat, her father cut off her fingers at the next joint; these segments fell into the sea and became big, bearded seals.",
      "locator": "Sedna",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sang ayah berusaha menenangkan petrel dengan melempar putrinya ke laut. Ketika ia berpegangan pada sisi perahu, ayahnya memotong jarinya pada ruas pertama; ujung jarinya jatuh ke laut dan menjadi anjing laut kecil. Ketika ia kembali berpegangan, ayahnya memotong pada ruas berikutnya; potongan itu menjadi anjing laut besar berjanggut.",
        "en": "The father tried to pacify the petrel by throwing his daughter overboard; when she clung to the boat he cut off her fingers at the first joint, and the fingertips became small seals, and at the next joint the segments became big, bearded seals."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sedna-c14",
      "source_id": "sedna-s2",
      "quote": "When she still clung to the side of the boat with the stumps of her fingers, her father cut them off at the last joint, and these segments turned into walruses. Sometimes the order of the creation of the sea animals is different, with whales being created first (the girl's nails are associated with baleen), followed by small seals and bearded seals.",
      "locator": "Sedna",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ketika ia masih berpegangan dengan sisa-sisa jarinya, ayahnya memotongnya pada ruas terakhir dan potongan itu menjadi walrus. Kadang urutan terciptanya hewan laut berbeda: paus tercipta lebih dulu (kuku sang gadis dikaitkan dengan balin), disusul anjing laut kecil dan anjing laut berjanggut.",
        "en": "When she still clung with the stumps of her fingers, her father cut them off at the last joint and these segments turned into walruses; sometimes the order differs, with whales created first (the girl's nails are linked to baleen), then small seals and bearded seals."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sedna-c15",
      "source_id": "sedna-s1",
      "quote": "Traditional Inuit tattoos often include lines across a woman's fingers, knuckles, or wrists, alluding to the story of where Sedna's fingers were cut. Some say Sedna denies access to the afterlife to women whose fingers weren’t tattooed.",
      "locator": "Kakiniit",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Tato tradisional Inuit sering memuat garis melintang di jari, buku jari, atau pergelangan tangan perempuan, merujuk pada kisah tempat jari Sedna dipotong. Sebagian orang mengatakan Sedna menolak jalan ke alam baka bagi perempuan yang jarinya tidak bertato.",
        "en": "Traditional Inuit tattoos often include lines across a woman's fingers, knuckles, or wrists, alluding to the story of where Sedna's fingers were cut, and some say Sedna denies access to the afterlife to women whose fingers were not tattooed."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sedna-c16",
      "source_id": "sedna-s1",
      "quote": "in art Sedna is usually depicted with the head and upper body of a woman and the tail of a marine mammal, similar to a mermaid.",
      "locator": "Depictions in art",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam seni, Sedna biasanya digambarkan berkepala dan berbadan atas perempuan dengan ekor mamalia laut, mirip putri duyung.",
        "en": "In art Sedna is usually depicted with the head and upper body of a woman and the tail of a marine mammal, similar to a mermaid."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "sedna-c17",
      "source_id": "sedna-s1",

        "en": "The trans-Neptunian object 90377 Sedna, discovered on November 14, 2003, is named after her."
      }
    }

    "id": "Kelinci bulan adalah tokoh mitos dalam cerita rakyat Asia Timur dan pribumi Amerika, berdasarkan tafsiran bercak gelap di Bulan sebagai kelinci atau hare.",
    "en": "The Moon rabbit is a mythical figure in East Asian and indigenous American folklore, based on reading the dark markings on the Moon as a rabbit or hare.",

    {
      "id": "Kelinci bulan, juga disebut hare bulan atau kelinci giok, lahir dari tafsiran yang mengenali bercak gelap di sisi dekat Bulan sebagai kelinci atau hare. Di Asia Timur kelinci itu dilihat menumbuk dengan lesung dan alu, tetapi isinya berbeda: di Tiongkok eliksir kehidupan atau obat bagi manusia fana dan kadang kue bulan, sedangkan di Jepang dan Korea beras manis untuk mochi atau kue beras.",
      "en": "The Moon rabbit, also called the Moon hare or Jade rabbit, arises from seeing a rabbit or hare in the dark patches on the Moon's nearer face.       "claim_ids": [

Legenda ini melahirkan beberapa festival di berbagai negara Asia.",
      "en": "An early Chinese source, the Chu Ci, records a hare on the Moon who ceaselessly pounds herbs for the immortals, and Han dynasty poets called it the \"Jade Hare\".         "id": "Kelinci menjadi persembahan bagi lelaki tua tak dikenal.",
        "en": "The rabbit makes itself an offering for an unknown old man."
      },
      "summary": {
        "id": "Dalam puisi itu, lelaki tua memulihkan kelinci, lalu membawa tubuhnya yang telah mati dan membaringkannya di istana bulan.",
        "en": "In the poem the old man makes the rabbit whole again, then takes its dead body and lays it to rest in the palace of the moon."
      },
      "claim_ids": [
        "moon-rabbit-c21",
        "moon-rabbit-c22"
      ]
    },
    {
      "title": {
        "id": "Kelinci dan Quetzalcoatl",
        "en": "The rabbit and Quetzalcoatl"
      },
      "role": {
        "id": "Kelinci menawarkan dirinya sebagai makanan.",
        "en": "The rabbit offers herself as food."
      },
      "summary": {
        "id": "Quetzalcoatl yang lapar dan lelah diselamatkan seekor kelinci yang menawarkan dirinya; tergerak, ia mengangkat kelinci itu ke Bulan dan menorehkan rupanya di sana.",
        "en": "A hungry, tired Quetzalcoatl is saved by a rabbit that offers herself; moved, he raises her to the Moon and imprints her image there."
      },
      "claim_ids": [
        "moon-rabbit-c12"
      ]
    }
  ],

  "learning_questions": [
    {
      "id": "Mengapa begitu banyak budaya melihat kelinci atau hare pada bercak gelap Bulan?",
      "en": "Why do so many cultures see a rabbit or hare in the dark markings on the Moon?"
    },
    {
      "id": "Apa persamaan dan perbedaan kisah Jataka dengan legenda Aztec tentang kelinci yang menawarkan diri?",
      "en": "What are the similarities and differences between the Jataka tale and the Aztec legend of the rabbit that offers itself?"

  "images": [],
  "sources": [

      "id": "moon-rabbit-s3",
      "author": "Ryokan (translated by Burton Watson)",
      "published": "1995",

      "accessed": "2026-10-06",
      "type": "other",
      "title": "The Rabbit in the Moon: A Poem by Zen Master Ryokan",

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c01",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s1",
      "quote": "In East Asian mythology, the rabbit is seen as pounding with a mortar and pestle, but the contents of the mortar differ among Chinese, Japanese, Korean, and Vietnamese folklore.",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam mitologi Asia Timur, kelinci dilihat sedang menumbuk dengan lesung dan alu, tetapi isi lesung berbeda-beda dalam cerita rakyat Tiongkok, Jepang, Korea, dan Vietnam.",
        "en": "In East Asian mythology the rabbit is seen pounding with a mortar and pestle, but the contents of the mortar differ among Chinese, Japanese, Korean, and Vietnamese folklore."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c02",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s1",
      "quote": "In some Chinese versions, the rabbit pounds medicine for the mortals and some include making of mooncakes.",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam sebagian versi Tiongkok, kelinci menumbuk obat bagi manusia fana dan sebagian memuat pembuatan kue bulan.",
        "en": "In some Chinese versions the rabbit pounds medicine for mortals, and some include the making of mooncakes."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c03",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s1",
      "quote": "Knowing only how to gather grass, the rabbit instead offered its own body by throwing itself into a fire the man had prepared. However, the rabbit was not burnt and the old man revealed that he was Śakra. Touched by the rabbit's virtue, he drew the likeness of the rabbit on the Moon for all to see.",
      "locator": "East Asian folklore",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Karena hanya tahu mengumpulkan rumput, kelinci menawarkan tubuhnya sendiri dengan melompat ke api yang disiapkan lelaki tua itu. Namun kelinci tidak terbakar dan lelaki tua itu mengungkapkan dirinya Śakra. Tersentuh oleh kebajikan sang kelinci, ia menggambar rupa kelinci itu di Bulan agar semua orang melihatnya.",
        "en": "Knowing only how to gather grass, the rabbit offered its own body by throwing itself into a fire the man had prepared; the rabbit was not burnt and the old man revealed himself as Śakra, who, touched by its virtue, drew the likeness of the rabbit on the Moon for all to see."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c04",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s1",
      "quote": "Moon folklore from certain Amerindian cultures of North America also has rabbit themes and characters.",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "scholarly-interpretation",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Cerita rakyat tentang bulan dari beberapa budaya Amerindian di Amerika Utara juga memuat tema dan tokoh kelinci.",
        "en": "Moon folklore from certain Amerindian cultures of North America also has rabbit themes and characters."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c05",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s1",
      "quote": "The Moon rabbit, Moon hare or Jade rabbit is a mythical figure in both East Asian and indigenous American folklore, based on interpretations that identify the dark markings on the near side of the Moon as a rabbit or hare.",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kelinci bulan, hare bulan, atau kelinci giok adalah tokoh mitos dalam cerita rakyat Asia Timur dan pribumi Amerika, berdasarkan tafsiran yang mengenali bercak gelap di sisi dekat Bulan sebagai kelinci atau hare.",
        "en": "The Moon rabbit, Moon hare or Jade rabbit is a mythical figure in both East Asian and indigenous American folklore, based on interpretations that identify the dark markings on the near side of the Moon as a rabbit or hare."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c06",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s1",
      "quote": "An early Chinese source called the Chu Ci, a Western Han anthology of Chinese poems from the Warring States period, notes that along with a toad, there is a hare on the Moon who constantly pounds herbs for the immortals.",
      "locator": "History",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sumber Tiongkok awal bernama Chu Ci, antologi puisi Han Barat dari zaman Negara-Negara Berperang, mencatat bahwa di samping seekor kodok, ada seekor hare di Bulan yang terus menumbuk ramuan bagi para makhluk abadi.",
        "en": "An early Chinese source, the Chu Ci, a Western Han anthology of poems from the Warring States period, notes that along with a toad there is a hare on the Moon who constantly pounds herbs for the immortals."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c07",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s1",
      "quote": "Han dynasty poets call the hare on the Moon the \"Jade Hare\" (玉兔 ) or the \"Gold Hare\" (金兔 ), and these phrases were used often, in place of the word for the Moon.",
      "locator": "History",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Para penyair dinasti Han menyebut hare di Bulan \"Hare Giok\" (玉兔) atau \"Hare Emas\" (金兔), dan ungkapan itu sering dipakai menggantikan kata untuk Bulan.",
        "en": "Han dynasty poets call the hare on the Moon the \"Jade Hare\" (玉兔) or the \"Gold Hare\" (金兔), and these phrases were often used in place of the word for the Moon."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c08",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s1",
      "quote": "In Chinese folklore, the rabbit, Yutu, is often portrayed as a companion of the Moon goddess Chang'e, constantly pounding the elixir of life for her and some show the making of cakes or rice cakes",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam cerita rakyat Tiongkok, kelinci bernama Yutu sering digambarkan sebagai pendamping dewi Bulan Chang'e, yang terus menumbuk eliksir kehidupan untuknya; sebagian menampilkan pembuatan kue atau kue beras.",
        "en": "In Chinese folklore the rabbit Yutu is often portrayed as a companion of the Moon goddess Chang'e, constantly pounding the elixir of life for her, and some versions show the making of cakes or rice cakes."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c09",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s1",
      "quote": "In the Buddhist Jataka tales, Tale 316 relates that a monkey, an otter, a jackal, and a rabbit resolved to practice charity on the day of the full moon (Uposatha), believing a demonstration of great virtue would earn a great reward.",
      "locator": "East Asian folklore",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam kisah Jataka Buddhis, Kisah 316 menceritakan seekor monyet, berang-berang, jakal, dan kelinci bertekad berderma pada hari bulan purnama (Uposatha), percaya bahwa menunjukkan kebajikan besar akan mendatangkan ganjaran besar.",
        "en": "In the Buddhist Jataka tales, Tale 316 relates that a monkey, an otter, a jackal, and a rabbit resolved to practice charity on the full-moon day (Uposatha), believing great virtue would earn a great reward."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c11",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s1",
      "quote": "This legend also gave rise to the Mid-Autumn Festivals of China, Tết Trung Thu of Vietnam, Tsukimi of Japan, and Chuseok of Korea, and Bon Om Touk in Cambodia, all of which celebrate the legend of the Moon rabbit.",
      "locator": "East Asian folklore",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Legenda ini juga melahirkan Festival Pertengahan Musim Gugur di Tiongkok, Tết Trung Thu di Vietnam, Tsukimi di Jepang, Chuseok di Korea, dan Bon Om Touk di Kamboja, yang semuanya merayakan legenda kelinci bulan.",
        "en": "This legend also gave rise to the Mid-Autumn Festivals of China, Tết Trung Thu of Vietnam, Tsukimi of Japan, Chuseok of Korea, and Bon Om Touk in Cambodia, all of which celebrate the legend of the Moon rabbit."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c12",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s1",
      "quote": "According to an Aztec legend, the god Quetzalcoatl, then living on Earth as a human, started on a journey ... a rabbit grazing nearby offered herself as food to save his life. Quetzalcoatl, moved by the rabbit's noble offering, elevated her to the Moon before returning her to Earth, imprinting her image in the Moon.",
      "locator": "Indigenous American folklore",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Menurut legenda Aztec, dewa Quetzalcoatl yang saat itu hidup di Bumi sebagai manusia memulai sebuah perjalanan ... seekor kelinci yang merumput di dekatnya menawarkan dirinya sebagai makanan untuk menyelamatkan nyawanya. Tergerak oleh persembahan mulia itu, Quetzalcoatl mengangkat sang kelinci ke Bulan sebelum mengembalikannya ke Bumi, dan menorehkan rupanya di Bulan.",
        "en": "According to an Aztec legend, Quetzalcoatl, living on Earth as a human, started a journey; a rabbit grazing nearby offered herself as food to save his life, and moved by the offering he elevated her to the Moon before returning her to Earth, imprinting her image in the Moon."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c14",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s1",
      "quote": "In a Cree cultural legend, a young rabbit wished to ride the Moon, but only the crane was willing to take him there. The trip stretched the crane's legs, leaving them elongated.",
      "locator": "Indigenous American folklore",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam legenda budaya Cree, seekor kelinci muda ingin menunggang Bulan, tetapi hanya burung bangau yang bersedia membawanya ke sana. Perjalanan itu meregangkan kaki sang bangau hingga memanjang.",
        "en": "In a Cree cultural legend a young rabbit wished to ride the Moon, but only the crane was willing to take him there, and the trip stretched the crane's legs, leaving them elongated."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c15",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s1",
      "quote": "Due to Tecciztecatl's cowardice, the other deities believed that the Moon should not be as bright as the Sun and threw a rabbit at Tecciztecatl's face to diminish his light.",
      "locator": "Indigenous American folklore",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Karena kepengecutan Tecciztecatl, para dewa lain menganggap Bulan tidak boleh seterang Matahari dan melemparkan seekor kelinci ke wajah Tecciztecatl untuk meredupkan cahayanya.",
        "en": "Because of Tecciztecatl's cowardice, the other deities believed the Moon should not be as bright as the Sun and threw a rabbit at Tecciztecatl's face to diminish his light."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c16",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s2",
      "quote": "In Asia, a white hare is said to inhabit the moon. An early version of the story of how the hare came to live on the moon can be found in the Jataka Tales from India concerning the incarnations of the Buddha before being born as Gautama Buddha (tale 316).",
      "locator": "Yuki Usagi",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Di Asia, seekor hare putih dikatakan mendiami bulan. Versi awal kisah bagaimana hare itu tinggal di bulan terdapat dalam Kisah Jataka dari India tentang inkarnasi Buddha sebelum lahir sebagai Gautama Buddha (kisah 316).",
        "en": "In Asia a white hare is said to inhabit the moon; an early version of the story of how the hare came to live there is in the Jataka Tales from India about the Buddha's incarnations before he was born as Gautama Buddha (tale 316)."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c17",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s2",
      "quote": "The man reveals that he is the deity Sakra and prevents the hare from being burned by the fire. Then he takes him to live on the moon for eternity so that his selflessness can be witnessed by the world.",
      "locator": "Yuki Usagi",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Lelaki itu mengungkapkan dirinya dewa Sakra dan mencegah hare itu terbakar. Lalu ia membawanya tinggal di bulan untuk selamanya agar ketidakegoisannya disaksikan dunia.",
        "en": "The man reveals that he is the deity Sakra and prevents the hare from being burned, then takes him to live on the moon for eternity so that his selflessness can be witnessed by the world."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c18",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s2",
      "quote": "In Japan this is the Jeweled Hare or the Jade Hare and has the symbolic meanings of charity and longevity.",
      "locator": "Yuki Usagi",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Di Jepang, hare dalam kisah ini adalah Hare Permata atau Hare Giok dan bermakna simbolis kedermawanan dan umur panjang.",
        "en": "In Japan the hare of this tale is the Jeweled Hare or the Jade Hare, with the symbolic meanings of charity and longevity."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c19",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s2",
      "quote": "In Chinese culture the hare in the moon is said to pound medicine in a mortar and pestle, but in Korea and Japan, he pounds sweet rice to make mochi treats. This gives the hare an association with the New Year, when eating mochi has a special place in the festivities.",
      "locator": "Yuki Usagi",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam budaya Tiongkok hare di bulan dikatakan menumbuk obat dengan lesung dan alu, tetapi di Korea dan Jepang ia menumbuk beras manis untuk membuat mochi. Ini memberi hare itu kaitan dengan Tahun Baru, ketika makan mochi punya tempat khusus dalam perayaan.",
        "en": "In Chinese culture the hare in the moon is said to pound medicine in a mortar and pestle, but in Korea and Japan he pounds sweet rice to make mochi, which gives the hare an association with the New Year, when eating mochi has a special place."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c20",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s2",
      "quote": "The hare in the moon is usually depicted as white.",
      "locator": "Yuki Usagi",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Hare di bulan biasanya digambarkan berwarna putih.",
        "en": "The hare in the moon is usually depicted as white."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c21",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s3",
      "quote": "This tale, retold by Zen monk-poet Ryokan (c. 1758–1831), draws on an old Chinese legend of a rabbit who lives in the moon. It is one of many Jataka tales, stories of Shakyamuni Buddha’s previous lives that illustrate acts of selflessness.",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "scholarly-interpretation",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah ini, yang dituturkan ulang penyair-biksu Zen Ryokan (sekitar 1758–1831), bersumber dari legenda Tiongkok kuno tentang kelinci yang tinggal di bulan. Kisah ini salah satu dari banyak Jataka, cerita kehidupan lampau Buddha Shakyamuni yang menggambarkan tindakan tanpa pamrih.",
        "en": "This tale, retold by the Zen monk-poet Ryokan (c. 1758–1831), draws on an old Chinese legend of a rabbit who lives in the moon, and it is one of many Jataka tales, stories of Shakyamuni Buddha's previous lives that illustrate acts of selflessness."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "moon-rabbit-c22",
      "source_id": "moon-rabbit-s3",
      "quote": "Then he made the rabbit whole again and gathering the dead body up in his arms, took it and laid it to rest in the palace of the moon.",
      "locator": "Poem text",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Lalu ia memulihkan kelinci itu dan, sambil memangku tubuh yang telah mati dalam pelukannya, membawanya dan membaringkannya di istana bulan.",
        "en": "Then he made the rabbit whole again and, gathering the dead body up in his arms, took it and laid it to rest in the palace of the moon."
      }
    }
  ],

  "traits": [],
  "short_description": {
    "id": "Ixchel adalah dewi jaguar tua dalam budaya Maya yang dikenal sebagai dewi kebidanan dan pengobatan.",
    "en": "Ixchel is an old jaguar goddess of the ancient Maya, tied to midwifery and medicine.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "ixchel-c02",
      "ixchel-c03"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Ixchel atau Ix Chel adalah nama abad ke-16 bagi dewi jaguar tua kebidanan dan pengobatan dalam budaya Maya kuno. Pada tahun 1500-an ia disebut \"dewi pembuat anak\" dan dewi pengobatan; pada bulan Zip, para tabib dan dukun merayakan pesta Ihcil Ixchel dengan mengeluarkan berhala kecil sang dewi. Ia sepadan dengan Toci, dewi bumi Aztec, dan dengan Dewi O dalam Kodeks Dresden, seorang wanita tua bertelinga jaguar.",
      "en": "Ixchel, or Ix Chel, was the sixteenth-century name of an elderly jaguar goddess of midwifery and medicine among the ancient Maya. In the 1500s she was called \"the goddess of making children\" and the goddess of medicine; in the month of Zip, physicians and shamans celebrated the feast Ihcil Ixchel and brought out little idols of the goddess. She corresponds to Toci, an Aztec earth goddess, and to Goddess O of the Dresden Codex, who is drawn as an old woman whose ears are those of a jaguar.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "ixchel-c01",
        "ixchel-c02",
        "ixchel-c03",
        "ixchel-c04"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Ikonografinya beragam: ular yang melilit dapat menjadi hiasan kepalanya, tulang bersilang dapat menghiasi roknya, dan ia kadang bercakar. Sebagai dewi jaguar ia dapat pula dibayangkan sebagai prajurit perempuan yang menakutkan bersenjata perisai dan tombak, dan dalam kodeks ia dikaitkan dengan dewa hujan. Satu sumber menggambarkannya sebagai perempuan renta bertaring yang juga punya wujud lebih ramah, muda dan cantik, ketika melambangkan kesuburan dan persalinan.",
      "en": "Her iconography varies: an entwined serpent may serve as her headdress, crossed bones may adorn her skirt, and she sometimes has claws. As a jaguar goddess she could equally be imagined as a fearsome female warrior with shield and spear, and in the codices she is linked to rain deities. One source describes her as a fanged, aged crone who also has a gentler, youthful and beautiful form when she stands for fertility and childbirth.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "ixchel-c08",
        "ixchel-c09",
        "ixchel-c10",
        "ixchel-c18"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Pada awal abad ke-16, perempuan Maya yang menginginkan pernikahan subur berziarah ke tempat suci Ix Chel di Pulau Cozumel, tempat seorang imam yang bersembunyi dalam patung besar memberikan ramalan. Hingga kini di Cozumel kuil Perawan Maria dan Ixchel sering dianggap satu dan sama. Satu-satunya mitos tentang Ixchel yang terlestarikan menyebut ia dan suaminya Itzamna memiliki tiga belas putra, dua di antaranya menciptakan langit dan bumi.",
      "en": "In the early 16th century Maya women who wanted a fruitful marriage made a pilgrimage to the sanctuary of Ix Chel on Cozumel, where a priest hidden inside a large statue gave oracles. On Cozumel today, shrines to the Virgin Mary and Ixchel are often treated as one and the same. The only preserved myth of Ixchel tells of thirteen sons born to her and Itzamna, her spouse, two of whom made heaven and earth.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "ixchel-c11",
        "ixchel-c12",
        "ixchel-c19"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": {
    "id": "Pada abad ke-16 para tabib dan dukun merayakan pesta Ihcil Ixchel bagi dewi pengobatan. Kini, di Pulau Cozumel, kuil bagi Perawan Maria dan dewi Ixchel dapat saling tertukar dan sering satu dan sama.",
    "en": "In the 16th century physicians and shamans celebrated the feast Ihcil Ixchel for the goddess of medicine. Today on Cozumel, shrines of the Virgin Mary and of the goddess Ixchel can be swapped for one another and frequently are the same shrine.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "ixchel-c01",
      "ixchel-c19"
    ]
  },
  "etymology": {
    "original_form": "Ixchel",
    "language": "Maya",
    "literal_meaning": {
      "id": "Maknanya tidak pasti; jika berasal dari Yucatán, chel dapat berarti \"pelangi\". Sumber lain menyebutnya bentuk feminin dari Chel.",
      "en": "Its meaning is uncertain; if it originated in Yucatán, chel could mean \"rainbow\". Another source calls it the feminine of Chel."
    },
    "claim_ids": [
      "ixchel-c05",
      "ixchel-c16"
    ]
  },
